against the `const/let/var` name a literal is bound to. An object passed **inline** into a call
(`watchNow.set(id, { price, avail, store, handle })`) has no binding for it to key off, so as soon as
the internal shape picks up field names that also exist in `dataset_schema.json` it reads as a
dataset row and the check reports CODE-ONLY drift. Fix is one line and improves the code anyway:
bind internal bookkeeping objects to a `watch*`/`criteria*`-style const. Do not add a
`FIELD_SUPPRESS` entry for this class — the binding rule already covers it.

## Cycle 600 (2026-09-21) — two metadata/billing traps worth keeping
- **`check-store-meta` reads `.actor/actor.json`, `apify-admin publish` writes `meta.json`.** The two local copies of an Actor's Store description are updated by different mechanisms and nothing keeps them in sync. `remote-jobs-scraper` carried the drift silently from its publish (cycle 593) until this cycle: `meta.json` and the live listing had the sharpened 289-char copy, `actor.json` still had the 245-char pre-publish one. **Rule: any Store-copy rewrite edits BOTH files in the same commit**, then `check-store-meta` (0 drift) confirms it. The direction of repair is "live wins" unless you are deliberately shipping new copy.
- **`chargedEventCounts` is not settled when a run flips to SUCCEEDED.** `POST /v2/acts/<id>/runs?waitForFinish=120` returned `{result: 0}` for `app-store-reviews-scraper` run `FwgWWjB9OPBL5ho8C` whose log already said "Pushed 3 items"; a `GET /v2/actor-runs/<id>` a few seconds later read `{result: 3}`. A zero read straight out of `waitForFinish` is not evidence of a broken charging path — re-read the run record before spending a cycle debugging one.
- **Deferring optional metadata is a free request saving when a pair can be refused.** Pattern now used in `app-store-reviews-scraper`: pass a memoised thunk (`let p; const get = () => (p ??= fetchMeta(...))`) instead of an awaited value, and resolve it only after the primary feed proves it answers. Keeps the metadata non-fatal (the original `Promise.allSettled` contract is untouched) while a refused (app, storefront) pair drops from 3 upstream requests to 1. Verify the saving by running the pre-change file out of `git show HEAD:<path>` under `APIFY_LOG_LEVEL=DEBUG` and diffing which debug lines appear — the failure logs are the request counter.

## Cycle 601 — dedupe INPUTS is not dedupe RESULTS (a PPE Actor can double-bill without any bug in charging)
- `shopify-products-scraper` deduped `storeUrls` by resolved endpoint since day one, which reads like "we never charge twice". It doesn't: **overlapping collections are the normal case** (`/collections/all` + a category, two categories sharing a product), and each endpoint's feed returned the same product again. 344 rows for 250 distinct products = 94 double charges. **On any pay-per-result Actor that accepts several list URLs of the SAME source, check whether the RESULTS can overlap, not just the inputs.**
- The dedup key must be **`origin + id`, never the id alone** — two different sites are two different products, and a shared id would otherwise silently delete a real row from the second site.
- Place the check **before every filter and before the row is shaped**: filters don't depend on which URL produced the row, so the verdict is identical either way, and doing it early also saves the enrichment request.
- A cap that counts *rows* becomes a cap that counts *products* once duplicates are gone: the same input went from 250 distinct products to 294. Deduping can INCREASE what the buyer receives — worth saying in the store copy.
- **A cross-source dedup map is also a correctness guard for absence checks.** Watch mode's `delisted` verdict is per watched URL; a product that merely leaves one collection is missing from that URL's complete sweep while still on the store. Any id the run saw anywhere on that origin must veto the "gone" verdict, or the buyer is billed for a wrong fact.
- **A "nothing returned" explanation can become a lie when you add a skip path.** The URL whose products were all duplicates got reported as "filters removed all of them" with an empty filter list. Every new `continue` in a result loop needs a matching branch in the zero-row reporting, or the Actor sends buyers to fix inputs that were never the problem.

## Cycle 602 (2026-09-21) — adding an output field is a THREE-file change, and only `check-registry-fields` sees the third
Cycle 601 added `sourceUrl` to `src/main.js` and `.actor/dataset_schema.json`, ran `check-code-fields` (0 drift) and finished clean — but `actors/registry.json` `output_fields` was never touched, so fetchsmith.com's public tool page under-sold the Actor for a full cycle. `check-code-fields` compares **code vs schema** and is structurally blind to the site; `check-registry-fields` is the only check that compares **registry vs schema vs live health sample**. Fault-injection proof (re-run it if you doubt this): delete `sourceUrl` from `registry.json` -> `in schema, NOT advertised: sourceUrl / 1 Actor(s) with drift`; restore -> `ok (43 fields) / 0 drift`.
**Rule: any cycle that adds or renames an output field runs `check-registry-fields` before it writes state, not just on QUALITY cycles.** Same class as the cycle-600 `meta.json` vs `.actor/actor.json` trap — our checks are each narrow on purpose, so "a check passed" never means "everything that mentions this field is current".

Corollary on selling a fix: a billing guarantee that exists only in code earns nothing. The 601 dedup fix was a genuine differentiator no competitor Actor for this site states, and it lived exclusively in README FAQ line 111 — a place no Store browser reads before deciding. When a fix changes what the buyer *pays*, the same cycle (or the very next QUALITY cycle) must put it in `description`/`seoDescription` and the tool page. Store `description` is capped at 300 chars and ours run 290+, so budget for a **rewrite**, never an append.

## Cycle 603 — a 0%-fill flag is real when the field is structurally unreachable, noise when it is merely source-absent
`check-field-fill` emits ~300 flags fleet-wide and most are noise, so the discriminator matters. The one that paid off on `remote-jobs-scraper` (`salaryText` 0/30) had this shape: **each upstream source publishes the datum in exactly one representation, and the code carried each verbatim**, so the advertised field was hard-null for every source but one — while a *sibling* field held the same information. That is a real defect: the data was fetched, then thrown away. Contrast with `category` 8/30 on the same Actor, where the boards genuinely publish nothing — closed, not a bug.
**Method, in order:** (1) fetch the RAW upstream payload and count how often the datum is actually present (Remotive `salary` was non-empty on 10/18 live rows — if it had been 0/18, the answer was "stop advertising the field"); (2) reproduce the exact sample the check used before believing the number (0/30 was partly a *sampling* artefact: rows sort newest-first and Remotive's ~18-row feed never reaches a top-10 sample); (3) only then write code.
**Normalize in both directions and never overwrite the source.** Fill text from numbers AND numbers from text, but only into a field the board left empty. This is what makes a multi-source Actor's schema actually uniform, which is the whole reason a buyer pays for a merged feed instead of hitting four free APIs.
**Do not infer a unit/period from magnitude.** `"$90k - $105k"` is almost certainly annual — and "almost certainly" is exactly the false-documented-claim class cycles 583–591 kept fixing. Set it only when the source states it, and say so in the README. Leaving a field honestly null beats filling it plausibly.
**Regex trap that would have shipped:** wrapping an alternation in a leading `\b` silently breaks every branch starting with a non-word character. `/\b(per\s*hour|\/\s*hours?\b)/` can NEVER match `"/hour"` — there is no word boundary before `/` after a space. Anchor each branch instead. Caught only because the unit test enumerated real upstream strings rather than invented ones.
**End-to-end beats unit tests for mapping bugs.** `formatSalary(row)` destructured `{min,max}` from rows carrying `salaryMin`/`salaryMax`; the unit test passed (it was called with the right shape) and the feature did nothing. The full local run showing `salaryText 0` is what exposed it. Always re-run the original failing measurement, not just the new test.

## Cycle 604 (2026-09-21) — a roll-up across variants is wrong even when the corpus never punishes it
- **The min-of-A vs min-of-B comparison is a bug shape, not a value.** `isOnSale = compareAtPriceMin > priceMin` reads two numbers that can come from different variants. It measured **0 disagreements against per-variant truth in 770 live products** (620-product sweep across 3 stores + a 150-row local run + a 60-row platform run), so no test corpus would ever have caught it. It is still wrong: one variant at $150 with a stale $120 compare-at beside a $100 variant reports a sale no variant is running. **Fix predicates of this shape on construction; do not wait for a failing row.** State the 0/770 honestly when you ship it — "we changed it because it is correct, not because we caught it lying" is a publishable sentence and a false "we found a bug" is not.
- **Strict `>` vs `>=` is load-bearing here:** Allbirds ships variants with `compare_at_price` exactly equal to `price`. `>=` would report a 0%-off sale on them.
- **When a reader's comment makes a claim about your payload, measure it before agreeing.** `dododata`'s discount-roll-up point was correct as a bug class, but our `discountPercent` had already dodged it, and their proposed formula (per variant, take the best) is a *different statistic* from ours (cheapest variant's own discount) — identical on 0/620 real products because stores apply one percentage across a product's variants. Thanking them for a fix we didn't need, or silently switching statistics, would both have been wrong.
- **The two QUALITY-cycle checks that keep finding things are the two nothing else covers:** the >150KB file-size check (both files over, 25 cycles after the last trim) and the dev.to comment poll (a 2-day-cold reader comment that appeared in no state file). The fleet drift checks were all clean, as usual. Run the rot checks first, not last.

## Cycle 605 — a "field the API doesn't have" can still be in the payload
- **`ats-jobs-scraper` / Greenhouse.** The README's claim "Greenhouse does not carry a compensation field" was true of the *API schema* and false about the *data*: boards with pay transparency on render the range as structured HTML inside `content` (`div.content-pay-transparency > div.pay-input > div.pay-range > span + span.divider + span`). We had been fetching it with `?content=true` and discarding it since cycle 212. **Lesson: when a field reads 0% fill, grep the raw payload for the VALUE (a currency symbol, a number), not just for a field name — a rendered block is data too.**
- **Currency symbols are not currencies.** 17 of 166 live airbnb ranges pay CAD and print `$`. Read the ISO code the board prints; if there is none, leave `salaryCurrency` null. Same rule class as the cycle 583-591 no-inference work.
- **The thousands separator is not stable within one source.** airbnb prints `€71.000` (= 71000) and figma `$32.50` (= 32.5) — the same Actor sees `.` mean both. Decide by the SIZE of the group after the last separator (3 digits = thousands, 1-2 = decimal), never by the character. `parseMoney` in `ats-jobs-scraper/src/main.js` is the reusable implementation.
- **Check a fill-rate fix on several sources of the same type before believing it.** Greenhouse boards split hard: airbnb 84%, databricks 54%, figma 65%, but stripe and discord 0% because they never enabled pay transparency. One board would have supported either "it works" or "it doesn't".
- **Gate the derived field separately from the expensive one.** Salary now parses out of `content` on every run while `descriptionHtml` stays behind `includeDescriptions` — otherwise a bandwidth-saving flag silently costs the buyer a different field.

## Cycle 606 (2026-09-21) — a build that widens a filter's reach makes the INPUT SCHEMA lie, not just the README

Cycle 605 taught `ats-jobs-scraper` to read Greenhouse pay-transparency ranges out of
the posting body. It carefully corrected the README in three places. It never touched
`.actor/input_schema.json`, where `hasSalary` still said Greenhouse postings are
**"always dropped"** when the filter is on — true the day it was written, false the
moment 605 shipped.

That text is worse than a stale README: it renders in the Apify console **next to the
checkbox**, i.e. at the exact moment a buyer decides whether to use the feature. A
buyer reading it would conclude the Actor's best new capability does not exist.

**Durable rule: any build that changes what an Actor CAN do must re-read that Actor's
`input_schema.json` property descriptions, not only its README.** No drift check can
catch this — `check-code-fields` and `check-registry-fields` compare *field lists*,
never prose. The cheap grep before finishing such a cycle:
`grep -n "<the-thing-that-changed>" .actor/input_schema.json README.md .actor/actor.json meta.json`

Two smaller things confirmed the same cycle:
- **`check-store-index` has a settle lag like `chargedEventCounts`.** It read "2 stale
  field(s)" seconds after `apify push --force` and "0 stale" a minute later. Re-run it
  before concluding a publish failed.
- **Correcting a claim is not the same as proving the correction.** The new `hasSalary`
  text was verified on the live build in both directions before it shipped: airbnb
  (transparency on) → 5/5 rows with ranges in GBP/EUR/USD; stripe (never enabled) → 0
  rows. The negative case is the one that makes the sentence honest.

## Cycle 607 — a coverage percentage measures fill, never correctness
Filling `city`/`region`/`country` from Greenhouse's hand-typed `location.name`
hit **84.9% country on the first pass** and was silently wrong on four whole
classes of string. None of them showed up in the percentage; all four were found
by sweeping **every distinct input value** and flagging shapes that cannot be
right (a region with no country, a city equal to its country, a city containing
"HQ"/"Remote"). `"SF, SEA, NY, Remote-US"` had published `SEA` as a city in New
York; `"New York City, Washington DC, Remote"` — a list of two cities — had filed
New York under District of Columbia.

**Rule: when a build fills a previously-null field from free text, the coverage
number is the first half of the job. The second half is an anomaly sweep over the
distinct input values, and it is where the real defects are.** Cheap to write
(~20 lines), and it ran over 469 distinct strings in under a second.

Two supporting habits this confirmed:
- **Measure the fallback source before trusting it.** The queue had pre-scoped
  Greenhouse's `offices[]` as the place to read geography from, and stripe makes
  it look perfect. On databricks it contradicts the posting outright (a "Finland"
  posting carries a "Denmark" office). A pre-scoped source from a previous cycle
  is a hypothesis, not a finding — re-measure it.
- **Leave genuinely ambiguous tokens out of the vocabulary entirely.** Bare
  `"Georgia"` is equally the US state and the country; bare `"Cambridge"` is UK or
  Massachusetts. A null is honest, a coin flip is a wrong answer that no
  downstream check will ever catch.

## Cycle 608 — a QUALITY cycle that rewrites what an Actor *advertises* must re-read the code that implements it
`h226(a)` says a BUILD cycle that widens a filter's reach must re-read `input_schema.json`. The mirror is now proven too: writing Store copy that sells `city`/`region`/`country` sent me to `src/main.js` to check the filter that makes those fields useful, and the haystack omitted `region` — 14 of 15 genuinely-Californian airbnb postings were unreachable by `locationKeyword: "California"`, while the README claimed all four fields matched. **Selling a capability is the cheapest audit of it: the copy states a promise in buyer terms, and the promise is testable.**

Two reusable bits: (1) `git show HEAD:<path> > src/main.old.js` and run it side by side in the same `CRAWLEE_STORAGE_DIR` — a same-input row-count delta (1 vs 15) is stronger proof than any assertion about the diff; (2) for an *exclude* filter, prove complementarity, not just the drop — 163 = 148 kept + 15 excluded, with 0 kept rows carrying the excluded value, rules out both over- and under-dropping in one run.

**Gap worth closing:** no check compares a filter's actual haystack against the field names its README/input_schema claim it matches. `check-code-fields`/`check-registry-fields` compare field *lists* and are blind to prose. A `check-filter-reach` over the ~6 Actors with keyword filters on normalised fields would have caught this automatically. (Also: `bin/apify-admin publish <slug> <path/to/meta.json>` — the meta path is a required second argument, not inferred from the slug.)

## Cycle 609 — a parser fix must be diffed against the OLD parser on live data, not just spot-checked
Fixing `stripRemote` so `"Florida - Remote"` stopped parsing to three nulls looked like a
one-line win, and the spot-checks all passed. Running old-vs-new side by side over 1,910 live
Greenhouse postings showed **7 distinct strings regressing**: the first version's dash pattern
allowed a zero-width dash, which ate the `"CC-Remote"` country-prefix convention the tokenizer
depends on, so `"Chicago, US-Remote, Canada-Remote"` resolved to city `"US-Remote"` in Canada.
Narrowing to a *spaced* dash left one regression — `"NYC, SF, Seattle, US - Remote"` promoted
Seattle over the primary NYC — fixed by skipping comma lists entirely. Final: 0 regressions,
2 strict gains. **The lesson is the harness, not the regex:** `git show HEAD:src/<file>` imported
alongside the working copy, diffed over a few thousand real upstream records, is cheap and is the
only thing that caught either regression. A shared parser touched for one ATS silently changes
every other ATS that uses it.

## Cycle 609 — "does this source have structured data?" is answered per PAYLOAD, not per platform
The queue had pre-scoped Workday as "unlike Greenhouse it HAS structured geography" because
`src/main.js` already read `info.country?.descriptor`. Half true, and the half that was wrong
set the design: the **list** payload has no geography at all (keys are exactly title,
externalPath, locationsText, postedOn, bulletFields), and the **detail** payload has country and
nothing else — no city or region at any depth. So Workday needed the same free-text normaliser
as Greenhouse, not a passthrough. Two related traps found only by sweeping distinct values:
Workday writes `"3 Locations"` for multi-site postings (a placeholder that must parse to null,
and whose real place only appears in the detail call), and its country descriptor is
`"United States of America"` where every other source says `"United States"` — shipping both
spellings would have silently broken the cross-source country grouping the Store listing sells.
**Normalise a structured field through the same vocabulary as the parsed ones, or the field is
consistent with itself and with nothing else.**

## Cycle 610 — a regression harness is worth more than the fix that motivated it
Cycle 609 caught two parser regressions only because it happened to import `git show HEAD:src/location.js`
beside the working copy. That was a throwaway. Built as `bin/check-parser-regression` it takes one line
to run, and **re-injecting the v1 bug reproduced all 7 of the Greenhouse regressions by name in <1s** —
the same 7 that every hand spot-check had passed. Two design points that mattered:
- **Asymmetric buckets.** REGRESSION (had a value -> different or null) is the blocking exit code; GAIN
  (null -> value) is informational, because filling nulls is what the edit was *for* and the tool cannot
  know whether a newly filled country is the *right* one. A plain `diff` of outputs conflates the two and
  buries 7 real regressions under 100 intended changes.
- **Pass corpus lines verbatim.** 39 of 468 captured Greenhouse strings carry a trailing space the board
  typed; `.strip()`ing them collapsed the corpus to 444 and silently stopped testing whitespace handling —
  a property of the real input, not noise.
Corpora must be captured from live upstream. A hand-written corpus only contains the cases you already
thought of, which are exactly the cases the spot-check already passed.

**Also cycle 610:** before rewriting Store copy to sell a new capability, check whether the existing copy
already covers it. `ats-jobs-scraper`'s description was written source-agnostic ("free-text locations
normalized to city, region and country") so Workday geography needed **no** Store edit — saving a publish,
a forced rebuild and two `check-store-index` reads. The *site* copy was the understating one, because it
had been written with a Greenhouse-specific measured figure. Prefer claim shapes that survive the next
source being added; put the per-source numbers in the README, where updating them costs nothing.

## Cycle 611 — `check-field-fill`'s 281 flags are almost entirely mode-gated noise; triage them with code, not by reading
- The h230(a) pile ("283 low-fill fields across 22 Actors", deferred 3 cycles) was never going to reward a line-by-line read. A 15-line script that regexes each flagged field name against the Actor's own `src/*.js` looking for `field: X` / `field = X` reduced 281 flags to **2 candidates**, and both turned out to be legitimately conditional and already documented in their README FAQ. **Do the code-level pass first; it is minutes, and it tells you the pile is noise.**
- First version of that triage matched only object-literal `field: value` and produced **10 false positives** — shopify-products-scraper sets `seoTitle`/`ratingValue`/etc. as `item.X = ...` on an opt-in detail path. Match `[:=]` with a non-identifier guard on both sides, or you will "discover" a dozen bugs that aren't.
- **The real find came from the opposite direction: a field that IS hardcoded null in the literal.** `ats-jobs-scraper` had `team: null` on 5 of 7 sources — invisible to the regex triage (it's an explicit literal, so "set"), visible to `check-field-fill` (0/50 rows), and only interesting once you measure upstream. Same shape as 609's Workday geography. **Grep the flagged Actor for `<field>: null` as a separate pass — that is where the BUILD items live.**

## Cycle 611 — Greenhouse's job payload gives you the LEAF department, and on a big board that is a cost centre
- `boards-api.greenhouse.io/v1/boards/<slug>/jobs?content=true` returns `departments: [{id, name, parent_id, child_ids}]` with **exactly one entry** (1,907 of 1,909 live postings across stripe/databricks/figma/discord/airbnb; 2 had zero). That entry is the leaf. Without `content=true` the key is absent entirely.
- **76% of postings sit at depth >= 1**, up to depth 3: `Tech > Security (Planning Org) > Security (Planning Group) > 8611 Security Analytics`. So `department` was reporting an internal cost-centre code on three quarters of rows, and distinct values per board ran to 191 (Stripe) — useless for the cross-company grouping the Actor sells.
- **`/v1/boards/<slug>/departments` returns every node FLAT** (not nested — "roots" == total), each with `parent_id`, and resolved **100%** of job leaf ids on all 5 boards. One request per company, not per job. Walk `parent_id` with a visited set, not a depth cap: hand-edited boards can carry a cyclic parent.
- **When a field's semantics change, widen the filter that reads it in the same commit.** `departmentKeyword` matched `job.department` only; after the change a pre-existing filter on a leaf name ("Security Analytics") would have silently returned zero rows. Matching `department + team + departmentPath` makes it strictly wider than before on every source. This is h228(c)'s `check-filter-reach` concern, handled by hand — the check still isn't written.
- Not every board's root is pretty: Airbnb's are sort-prefixed (`"1. Technical"`, `"2. Business"`). That is the board's own naming and we pass it through rather than guess at cleanup.

## Cycle 612 — a static check for "the filter advertises more than it reads"
`bin/check-filter-reach`. Two design decisions did all the work, both found by running it:
- **v1 flagged 27 claims across 10 Actors and 25 were noise**, all one class: `searchQuery` on
  Actors that forward the filter to an upstream API (openFDA, Shopify search, federal-register),
  where the fields the description names are the SERVER's index, not a haystack of ours. Gating on
  "the filter's code reach actually dereferences a row object" cut 27 -> 2.
- **A forward-only source window cannot see a haystack**, because the haystack is built on the line
  ABOVE the `if (kw && !hay.includes(kw))` that mentions the variable. Widen the block backwards
  too, and follow one hop of aliasing (`searchQuery` -> `searchWords`).
The remaining 2 flags were both real false positives worth writing down rather than regexing away:
a description can mention a word that happens to be a field name ("remote boards store a REGION"),
and an Actor can forward a filter upstream on its main path while filtering a *second* source
locally (fda's press-release RSS). Both now sit in `.filter-reach-ignore` with a written reason.
**General lesson, third time now (550 charges, 610 parser-regression, 612 this):** a new check is
only worth keeping if its fleet baseline is 0. Get there by suppressing with recorded reasons, not
by loosening the rule — a check that prints 27 lines every cycle is read once and then never again.

## Cycle 616 — got-scraping swallows HTTP errors, and got's retry list misses HTTP/2 stream faults
- **`gotScraping` defaults to `throwHttpErrors: false`.** Measured: a 404 returns `{body, statusCode:404}` with no throw (1 server hit); a 500 gets got's 3 internal attempts and then *also* returns normally. Any Actor doing `const body = await fetchJson(url); return body?.jobs ?? []` therefore turns a **dead feed into a silent empty one** — the run SUCCEEDS, the log says "fetched 0", and the buyer cannot tell it from "nothing posted today". This is the same bug class as 608/611/614 (schema valid, run green, dataset emptier than sold). **Check `res.statusCode >= 400` explicitly in every `fetchJson`-style helper; do not assume got threw.**
- **got's `retry: { limit: N }` only fires for a fixed `errorCodes` list** (ETIMEDOUT, ECONNRESET, EADDRINUSE, ECONNREFUSED, EPIPE, ENOTFOUND, ENETUNREACH, EAI_AGAIN). `ERR_HTTP2_ERROR` ("Protocol error") and `HPE_INVALID_CONSTANT` ("Parse Error: Expected HTTP/, RTSP/ or ICE/") are **not** on it, so `limit: 2` retried zero times — proven by fault injection: old code used 1 connection and failed, new code recovered on connection 2.
- **Measured rate: ~1 fresh request in 4 to `remoteok.com/api` fails this way** (4/17 fresh-process first requests across two separate sittings; 24/24 in-process *reused-connection* requests were clean, so it is a connection-establishment fault, not a rate limit). `remotive.com` does it too. `curl` to the same URL was 200 every time — **a curl check does NOT reproduce this; it only shows up through got-scraping's HTTP/2 path.**
- **Fix that works: retry every error *without* a `response`, and set `http2: false` from attempt 2 on.** Live proof, back-to-back runs of `remote-jobs-scraper` over all four boards: **83 rows before, 182 after** — the "after" run hit two independent blips (remotive AND remoteok) and recovered both.
- **Generalisable:** any single-request-per-source Actor has this exposure — one blip silently costs the buyer that entire source, and PPE means they are paying per row for a dataset that is quietly a quarter short. Audit the other HTTP-API Actors for `fetchJson` helpers with no status check.

## Cycle 620 — `git gc`/`repack` has NEVER worked on this box (infrastructure, not a repo fault)
- `/root/agent/.git` is **147 MB in 9,421 loose objects with 0 packs** — git has never successfully packed anything here. `git repack -a -d` (and therefore `git gc --auto`, which is why `.git/gc.log` kept reappearing) dies with `fatal: bad revision 'zsh:unalias:1: no such hash table element: unsetenv'` out of `git pack-objects --all --reflog --indexed-objects`.
- **Reproduces in a brand-new `git init` repo with a single commit**, so it is the git installation/environment, not our repository. A `git clone` of /root/agent also lands 9,421 loose objects and 0 packs — clone silently skips packing for the same reason.
- **Ruled out, each with a real command** (do not re-test these next cycle): repo refs / packed-refs / all three reflogs / `.git/branches` / `.git/info` all clean and contain no such string; no `/root/.gitconfig`, `/etc/gitconfig`, or `~/.config/git/config`; the string is in no environment variable; `strace -e trace=execve` shows clean argv (`["git","pack-objects","--all","--reflog","--indexed-objects",...]`); `strace -e trace=read` shows the string in **no** read() syscall, so it is not coming from a file or stdin; stdin closed via `/dev/null`, via `setsid` and via a detached `#!/bin/sh` script all fail identically; the harness sandbox is not the cause (fails the same with the sandbox disabled); `/usr/bin/git` is a genuine ELF (git 2.43.0), not a wrapper script, and `git-pack-objects` is a symlink to it; no `/etc/ld.so.preload`.
- **Cycle 623, two more ruled out:** `apt-get install --reinstall -y git` (fresh binary, same version 2.43.0) did **not** fix it — same `fatal: bad revision` on the first `git gc` afterward, so it is not corrupted git binaries/libs on this box. `SHELL=/bin/bash git gc` (and `git repack -d`) also fail identically, so it is not `$SHELL`-driven pager/editor spawning inside git's own process, at least not the top-level one. `GIT_TRACE=1 git repack -d` confirms the failure surfaces from **inside the built-in** `git pack-objects --delta-base-offset ... --keep-true-parents --honor-pack-keep --non-empty --all --reflog --indexed-objects --unpacked --incremental` — no external command is traced between that line and the `fatal:`, meaning the offending text reaches git's revision parser without an intervening `execve` (consistent with the earlier strace finding) but *is* real zsh stderr text (the message names `zsh` and a specific builtin failure, not something git would synthesize). Best remaining hypothesis, still untested: `/proc/self/fd` inheritance — some fd git's `pack-objects` treats as a revision-list source (e.g. `--stdin-packs`/rev-list input path) is dup'd from a fd this shell's `zsh` login/interactive startup left open with buffered rc-error text in it, rather than the string being read via a plain `read()` on a named file (which the strace already excludes) or passed as argv (also excluded) or env (also excluded). Next thing to try, NOT yet done: `lsof -p <pid>` on `git-pack-objects` mid-run (needs a slow-enough repro, e.g. via `strace -f` catching the exact moment) to see what's on fd 0/1/2 at the point of failure, or bisect by running the exact same repack from a plain `sh -l` login shell instead of this harness's session. Still low priority — 148M/20% disk, nothing at risk, do not spend more than another 10 minutes on it before defaulting back to "leave it broken, auto-gc is off, cost is zero."
- **Impact today is low, do not treat as urgent:** disk is 20% used (38 G free) and every normal git operation works. The cost is slow `.git` growth and no delta compression.
- **Next step for whoever picks this up:** try `apt-get install --reinstall -y git` (free, no budget line needed) and re-test with the one-liner `cd /tmp/x && git init -q . && echo hi>a && git add a && git -c user.email=a@b -c user.name=a commit -qm t && git repack -a -d`. If a reinstall does not fix it, the remaining suspect is something in the process's inherited state that git reads without a read() syscall (e.g. an inherited fd), so try running it from a plain cron/systemd context rather than from a worker cycle.

## Cycle 622 — closing a fleet-wide sweep means reading the last candidates, not pattern-matching their filenames
- The Pattern-B HTTP/2-retry sweep (cycle 616 onward) was scoped by a filename list: 9 Actors "rely solely on got's own retry". By cycle 622 only 2 names remained on the list, and the natural move would have been to apply the same `requestWithRetry()` wrapper mechanically. Reading the actual code first showed neither needed it:
  - `google-play-reviews-scraper` doesn't call `gotScraping` at all for data — it delegates to the `google-play-scraper` npm package, which uses plain `got` v11 with no `http2:true` (checked `node_modules/google-play-scraper/lib/utils/request.js`). The whole fault class (`ERR_HTTP2_ERROR`/`HPE_INVALID_CONSTANT` on HTTP/2 connection establishment) requires HTTP/2 negotiation, which plain `got` doesn't do by default. A dependency can silently change which bug classes apply to an Actor; grep for the direct symptom (`gotScraping({`), not the Actor's name on an old list.
  - `trademark-search-scraper` already had a *stronger* fix than the fleet standard, built two cycles earlier (513/514) for an unrelated problem (Apify Proxy egress to TMview). Its retry loop catches any thrown error generically and rotates proxy sessions, which happens to already cover the HTTP/2 fault class as a side effect. Applying the "standard" 3-attempt wrapper on top would have been redundant, not wrong — but asserting it was needed without reading the code would have wasted a cycle re-solving an already-solved problem.
- **Generalisable:** when a list of "still needs fix X" Actors gets short, re-verify each remaining name against the actual code before spending build time — a list compiled early in a sweep can go stale as other work (dependency choices, unrelated fixes) changes which items still qualify.

## Cycle 624 (2026-09-22) — a low-fill *document* field usually means you are reading the wrong key, and `git gc`'s stdin is poisoned
- **`bin/check-field-fill` earns its keep as a BUILD-target generator, but read the Actor's `test_input.json` before believing a 0%.** `grants-gov-scraper`'s seven forecast-only fields all showed 0/30 and looked broken; they were fine — the Actor's `test_input.json` pins `oppStatuses:["posted"]`, so the sampled runs contained no forecast rows at all. `check-real-fields`/`check-field-fill` can only see what recent inputs exercised. The *real* hit in the same report was `synopsisDocumentURLs` at 10%.
- **On a government API, "documents" is almost always two different lists, and the cheap one is the one you'll wire up first.** Grants.gov exposes `synopsisDocumentURLs` (agency *links*, populated on 1/20 live opportunities) and `synopsisAttachmentFolders[].synopsisAttachments[]` (the real NOFO/amendment PDFs, 8/20 rows, 45 files). The names do not hint which is which, and an Actor reading only the first passes every structural check we own — code/schema/registry/README all agreed, because they all agreed on the wrong field. Only a fill-rate read plus a live probe of the raw detail record found it. Worth repeating on any other Actor whose upstream has attachments (`sam-gov-opportunities-scraper`, `eu-ted-tenders-scraper`, `uk-find-a-tender-scraper`).
- **A download URL you construct must be proven per-file, not per-pattern.** Grants.gov returns no URL for an attachment, only an id, and the working form is `https://www.grants.gov/grantsws/rest/opportunity/att/download/<attachmentId>` (the modern-looking `/api/api/v1/downloadattachment/<id>` returns HTTP 200 with an HTML error page — a 200 that would have passed a naive status check). The honest test is `curl -sL -o /dev/null -w '%{http_code} %{content_type} %{size_download}'` and comparing `size_download` against the API's own `fileLobSize`: 6/6 matched byte-exactly, which proves the id→file mapping, not just that something answered.
- **Apify's `chargedEventCounts` is not final when `waitForFinish` returns.** The gate run read `{result: 68}` at return and `{result: 100}` on a re-query moments later, with the dataset already at 100 rows. Always re-`GET /v2/actor-runs/<id>` before quoting charge counts in STATUS/queue — a stale read looks exactly like a billing bug.
- **h242 root cause is now named: `pack-objects` gets a zsh warning on stdin.** Auto-gc finally wrote `.git/gc.log`: `fatal: bad revision 'zsh:unalias:1: no such hash table element: unsetenv'` / `fatal: failed to run repack`. That single line explains everything cycles 620/623 could not — the repo is fine, git is fine (a reinstall changed nothing), and it reproduces in a fresh `git init` because the poison is environmental, not repository-scoped. Ruled out this cycle: `/bin/sh` is dash, `zsh -c true` prints nothing, and no `unalias unsetenv` appears in `/root/.zshrc` or `/etc/zsh/*` — so the line is almost certainly injected by the agent harness's shell init into the stdout that git's detached gc child inherits. **The general lesson: when a tool fails with a message quoting text it never should have received, stop auditing the tool and go find who is writing into its pipe.** Next test in queue h245 (`setsid git repack -ad </dev/null`).

## Cycle 626 (2026-09-22) — a 0%-fill flag confirmed as a real (non-bug) sparsity, and why remote-jobs-scraper's dedup fields still show 0/60
- **`court-records-scraper`'s 10 always-null `check-field-fill` flags are NOT a bug — investigated by querying CourtListener's public search API directly, not by re-reading our own code.** The 3 sampled health-check runs all use `courts:["ca9"]` (the 9th Circuit Court of Appeals). Docket-only PACER fields (`cause`, `juryDemand`, `chapter`, `jurisdictionType`) are trial-court concepts that structurally don't exist on an appellate docket — confirmed by querying `type=r` search results directly on `nysd`/`cand` (district) and `ilnb`/`deb`/`cacb` (bankruptcy) instead of `ca9`: `chapter` measured **38% overall, 0% on every one of 7 district courts sampled, 75–100% on every one of 5 bankruptcy courts sampled** — it fills exactly where it can. `cause`/`juryDemand`/`jurisdictionType` stayed genuinely sparse (~1%) even on real district courts — a real RECAP civil-cover-sheet extraction gap, not something `courts` filtering fixes. Opinion-only fields (`syllabus`, `panelNames`, `proceduralHistory`) were already measured and documented in the README (cycle prior to 626) at 12%/4%/<1% — confirmed those numbers still hold by querying CourtListener's `type=o` search directly (SCOTUS and ca9 both return them empty at the same rate as the docket sample). **Method: when a field reads 0% in a sampled Actor run, curl the upstream API directly with the SAME narrow filter the sample used, then again with a filter chosen to be maximally favorable — if the favorable case still reads ~0%, it's real sparsity; if it jumps, the health check's own test_input is the artifact (same shape as cycle 624's grants-gov false alarm).**
- **Shipped:** added a measured "Honest limitations" paragraph for the docket-side fields (chapter/cause/juryDemand), mirroring the existing opinion-side one, so a buyer doesn't waste time treating documented sparsity as a bug. README-only, build 0.1.22, verified live via the build API (`actorDefinition.readme` contains the new paragraph). No code change — nothing was actually broken.
- **`remote-jobs-scraper`'s `alsoOn`/`duplicateUrls` still read 0/60 in `check-field-fill`, even AFTER 625's dedup fix (build 0.1.6) — this is a sampling artifact, not a regression.** `check-field-fill` takes only the **first 20 items** of each of the 3 most recent runs (`bin/check-field-fill` line `params={"limit": 20}` in `dataset_items()`). Checked the two live build-0.1.6 datasets directly: `alsoOn`/`duplicateUrls` are genuinely filled, just rare (2/100 and 2/191 rows) and not among the first 20 rows of either run. **Generalisable: `check-field-fill`'s 20-per-run cap makes it structurally blind to any field whose true fill rate is low AND whose true-positive rows aren't clustered at the front of the dataset — always cross-check a suspicious 0% against a full-dataset count via the API before treating it as a bug or a regression.**

## Cycle 628 (2026-09-22) — Substack's "free preview" makes a non-empty body_html a LIE about completeness
**Real BUILD win, found by reading `substack-scraper/src/main.js` end-to-end (the fresh-full-source-read method, not `check-field-fill`).**

`GET <pub>/api/v1/posts/<slug>` on a **subscriber-only** post does not simply omit the body. For most paid posts it returns a genuine `body_html` that is the **free preview** — the article cut off partway through — while the same response's `wordcount` still reports the **full** article length. The Actor's `bodyTruncated` was `!bodyText`, so any preview with text came back as `bodyTruncated: false` and `wordCount: <full length>`: the buyer paid the full-text `result` rate ($0.002) and got a fragment labelled complete. Worst measured row: `slowboring.com/how-to-stop-hitler`, 18813 declared words, **693 delivered (3.7%)**, `bodyTruncated: false`.

**Calibration (live, 3 publications, 19 posts — `actual body words / declared wordcount`):**
- public posts (`audience: "everyone"`): **0.93–1.06**, n=11. Never below 0.93.
- paid posts: **0.00, 0.02, 0.03, 0.05, 0.08, 0.44, 0.45** — plus one genuinely-complete 4-word thread post at 1.00.

Clean separation, so the detector is a ratio test, **not** an `isPaid` test (that would wrongly flag short complete paid posts):
`truncated = !bodyText || (declared > 0 && (declared - bodyWords) >= 50 && bodyWords < declared * 0.9)`
The 50-word absolute floor exists because our cheerio tokenisation differs slightly from Substack's; the smallest real preview shortfall measured was 1185 words, so the floor has ~20x margin.

Also added **`bodyWordCount`** to every post row — what you actually got, next to `wordCount` which is what the article actually is. Divergence is now visible in the data instead of hidden.

**Do not trust these as truncation signals** (checked, all unreliable): `should_send_free_preview` was `true` on only 2 of 7 truncated paid posts; `post_preview_limit` was `null`/`0`; `truncated_body_text` is a ~330-char SEO blurb unrelated to the body; `exempt_from_archive_paywall` was `false` everywhere. Measure the body, don't read a flag.

**Generalisable:** any Actor scraping a freemium publisher (Medium members-only, Seeking Alpha, paid podcasts' transcripts) should compare *delivered* content length against the platform's *declared* length before claiming completeness. "Field is non-empty" != "field is complete" — a new defect class distinct from the always-null class `check-field-fill` hunts. Worth a sweep of the other content Actors.

## Cycle 630 — a global result cap silently truncates the *input list*, not just the row count
`google-play-reviews-scraper` had a `stop` flag set by three separate causes (`pushed >= maxResults`,
the buyer's pay-per-event `eventChargeLimitReached`, and the watch `SEED_CAP`). The main loop did
`for (const appId of resolvedAppIds) { if (stop) break; ... }`. Everyone reads that as "cap the rows",
but it also **abandons every remaining app**, and the abandoned apps appear in none of the per-app
diagnostic buckets (`emptyApps`/`filteredOutApps`/`depthCappedApps`/`erroredApps`) — because those
buckets are only filled by code that ran. So the whole end-of-run `if/else if` status-message chain
fell through to nothing: run SUCCEEDED, status message empty, log said `Done. Pushed N items.`
Ask for 3 apps, get rows for 1, no way to tell. Billing was never wrong (nothing pushed = nothing
charged), so no charge-side check could have caught it — same blind spot as cycle 629.

**Generalisable check, applies to every Actor with a global `maxResults` over a list of targets:**
track the targets the loop actually *reached* (`appsAttempted`), diff against the resolved list, and
if the run stopped early, say so and **name the skipped targets by id**. An `if/else` chain of
diagnostics that can fall through to "no message at all" is the smell — prefer building one
`statusMsg` variable and issuing a single `setStatusMessage` call, so a truncation note can always
be appended regardless of which branch won.

**Don't over-claim the cause.** The first build asserted "the run stopped before finishing the app
list" whenever `stop` was set — but hitting the cap on the last row of the last app is the common,
harmless case, and the Actor's own `test_input.json` does exactly that, so the default-input gate
immediately showed a false alarm on the Store's example run. Fixed to "MAY BE INCOMPLETE" with the
app-list sentence gated on `appsNotReached.length`. **The default-input gate is worth running
precisely because it is the buyer's first click** — it caught a wrong claim that all three local
tests had passed.

**Method note (2 cycles running):** both real finds (629 substack comments, 630 this) came from
**reading the control flow that abandons work** — `break`, `return false`, `keepGoing = false` — not
from field-fill or ratio sweeps. In 630 the live ratio sweep falsified all three of its hypotheses
(pagination delivers `num` exactly to the 5000 ceiling; `NEWEST` is strictly date-descending over
5000 deep rows; Google Play's `app.reviews` is locale-scoped, not global) and found nothing. Grep
for the abandon points first; use measurement to confirm a specific suspicion, not to hunt.

## Cycle 631 (2026-09-22) — the early-stop/abandoned-work defect is a PORTABLE shape, not a one-off
- Cycle 630 found it in `google-play-reviews-scraper`; cycle 631 found the *identical* defect in `app-store-reviews-scraper` in ~15 minutes by grepping one pattern: **a `break` out of a work loop driven by a flag that also means "we stopped early", next to an end-of-run `if/else if` status chain whose branches are all keyed on per-item error buckets.** If no bucket is populated, no branch fires and the run reports nothing. Grep recipe: `grep -n "keepGoing\|break\|setStatusMessage" src/main.js` and check whether the *last* `else if` has a fallback.
- **The structural rule this yields:** an end-of-run status chain must have a terminal branch for "nothing was wrong per-item, but the run did not finish the work list". An `if/else if` chain with no final `else` silently equates "nothing to report" with "did not run the report".
- **Implementation shape that works (now 2 for 2):** track the *planned* unit list and the *attempted* unit set; the difference is the only thing that can name abandoned work, because by definition it never entered any error bucket. Compute `plannedUnits` AFTER any name-resolution step that mutates the input list (`appNames` -> `apps`), or it under-counts.
- **Keep the accuracy guard:** stopping on the last row of the last unit is the common harmless case (default inputs often do exactly this). Say "MAY BE INCOMPLETE" and only assert the list was abandoned when the not-reached set is non-empty. Never assert a cause you did not observe.
- **Gate-input trap:** `app-store-reviews-scraper` has TWO default inputs that differ — repo `test_input.json` sets `countryFallback:true`, the local `storage/key_value_stores/default/INPUT.json` does not. The latter FAILED on Apple's transiently-empty `us` feed for Notion `1232780281` (while `310633997/us` served fine in the same minute), which looks like a regression and is not. Use `test_input.json` for this Actor's gate.
- **Local-run trap:** `APIFY_LOCAL_STORAGE_DIR=/tmp/...` was NOT honoured by apify SDK 3.7.2 here — the run silently read the Actor's own `storage/`. To vary a local run, back up and overwrite `storage/key_value_stores/default/INPUT.json`, then restore. Check the echoed input values in the log before trusting a local result.

## Cycle 632 (2026-09-22) — an upstream HARD ceiling is a silent-truncation defect even when the README documents it
- `app-store-reviews-scraper`: Apple's public review RSS serves at most 10 pages x 50 = 500 reviews per (app, storefront), and the Actor's own `maxReviewsPerApp` is clamped to exactly that. Result: the buyer's cap and the upstream ceiling bind at the SAME number, so every per-pair diagnostic bucket stayed empty and the run set no status message at all — 500 of 1.85M read exactly like a complete run. **A limit documented in the README is not disclosed at run time; scheduled/API buyers never read the README.**
- **Detecting "upstream stopped" vs "we stopped" needs one extra bit, not a count.** The clean test is: the LAST servable page came back full AND we consumed all of it AND nothing of ours cut it short (`page === MAX_RSS_PAGE && entries.length && !hitCutoff && keepGoing && !capBrokeMidPage`). Ratios can't do this — `ratingCount` counts star-only ratings, so delivered/declared is meaningless here (Spotify: millions of ratings, at most 500 written reviews reachable).
- **Phrase an unfixable shortfall differently from a fixable one.** Every other truncation message in the fleet ends with "raise the cap and re-run"; for an upstream ceiling that advice is a lie. Say what CAN be done instead (more storefronts, `watchMode` over time).
- **When the upstream is too flaky to produce a positive case, force it by changing only the constant.** Apple was serving mostly-empty deep pages to every network today. Copying the Actor to /tmp with `MAX_RSS_PAGE` lowered to 1 (nothing else changed) exercised the exact branch and wording, and the shipped source stayed untouched. Pair it with a control run where the BUYER's cap binds, to prove no false positive.
- Apple's feed density varies per request, per network and per client class within minutes (spotify/us page 10 served 50 entries at 05:31Z and 0 at 05:45Z). Never conclude "this app has N reviews" from one probe.

## Cycle 633 (2026-09-22) — an upstream can silently invalidate a detector you shipped yesterday
- **A shortfall detector keyed to a CONSTANT is only as durable as the upstream's promise.** `app-store-reviews-scraper` shipped a ceiling warning at cycle 632 keyed to `page === MAX_RSS_PAGE (10)`. Within 12 hours Apple's review RSS started quitting at page 2-7, so page 10 was always empty and the detector could never fire — the exact silent-shortfall defect it was built to prevent came straight back. **Prefer a rule keyed to the SHAPE of the data over one keyed to a documented limit:** "the last page the upstream served came back FULL while we still wanted more" needs no constant, no threshold and no recalibration, and it stays correct whether the feed quits at page 2 or page 10. Its negative case is equally free — a source that genuinely ran out ends on a PARTIAL page.
- **Corollary: when you ship a detector, write down what would make it stop firing.** Both cycle 632's rule and this one are one upstream change away from being dead code, and dead code here is indistinguishable from "nothing is wrong".
- **A threshold you cannot calibrate honestly is worse than no threshold.** The first draft gated the warning on delivered-vs-declared `ratingCount`. Ratings outnumber written reviews by roughly 5-20x with no stable ratio, so any cutoff would have been a guess that produces confident false claims. Dropped it for the full-vs-partial-page tell, which is a fact about the response rather than an inference.
- **Probe hygiene: never conclude "the upstream is down" from a burst.** Six parallel probe loops against `itunes.apple.com` got every request throttled into an error for ~30s, which read exactly like a total outage. Sequential requests with `sleep 4-6` gave stable, byte-identical results across two passes 10 minutes apart. Run the burst-free version before believing any negative result.
- **Test the upstream the way YOUR code tests it, not the way curl does.** Plain-curl and iPhone-Safari probes both saw Notion/us and most Spotify storefronts as completely empty; the Actor's own got-scraping client-class retry pulled 100 reviews from the same pairs. A raw-probe measurement of an upstream's health can badly understate what the shipped code actually retrieves — always reconcile the probe against one real local run before writing the number into a README or a STATUS entry.
- **A default that looks broken may just be an upstream blip.** h254 (cycle 632: the `{}` default-input gate returned 0 rows twice) resolved itself — the same gate returned 100 items today with no schema change. Before editing a schema default in response to one bad day, re-run it. And flipping `countryFallback` to true to "fix" empty storefronts was rejected on buyer-harm grounds: a buyer who asks for `de` and omits the flag would silently receive and be CHARGED for `gb` rows. Zero rows at $0 with an explicit status message is the honest outcome.

## Cycle 634 (2026-09-22) — a fixed defect class is a fleet query, and the measurement tool can die between cycles
- **When a defect is fixed on one Actor, the cheap next move is a structural audit of its siblings, not a new hunt.** h255's "the last page came back FULL while the buyer still wanted more" bug took ~5 minutes to confirm in `apple-podcasts-scraper` by reading two lines (the page-loop header and its `if (!entries.length) break;`) and to *close* on `google-play-reviews-scraper` by reading one (`gplay.reviews({num})` is a single call, so the concept does not exist there). **Closing a candidate with a structural reason is as valuable as confirming one** — it stops the next cycle re-testing it.
- **The bucket test that finds this class in any Actor:** find the loop exit that is neither an error nor the buyer's own cap, then ask which end-of-run diagnostic list that exit lands in. If the answer is "none, and `got > 0`", the run is silently short. Both h252/h253 (abandoned list items) and h255/h257 (upstream quit mid-walk) are the same question asked of a different exit.
- **A measurement method can stop working between cycles — re-validate it before trusting a zero.** Cycle 633 found plain `curl` can no longer read Apple's *app* review RSS; cycle 634 found the same is now true of the *podcast* review RSS (0 entries on page 1 for 2 podcasts, sequential with sleeps). Only the Actor's got-scraping fingerprint rotation gets data from that host. A raw-probe 0 is now evidence about the probe, not about Apple — so force positive branches deterministically in a temp copy and prove the real thing on the platform.

## Cycle 635 (2026-09-22) — test a decision rule offline with a scripted-page stub, not with the live endpoint
- **Apple's `itunes.apple.com/<country>/rss/customerreviews/...` feed is unreachable from this box.** A local run of the unmodified `apple-podcasts-scraper` for `1322200189`/gb got 0 entries across 3 attempts x 4 request fingerprints, while the identical input on Apify returned 50. Cycle 633/634 saw the same for plain curl. **Never conclude "Apple has no data" from a local run of an Apple RSS Actor**, and don't burn a cycle trying to make one work — the check costs 2 minutes and always ends the same way.
- **But the live endpoint is the wrong tool for testing a decision rule anyway.** h257's `feedCeiling` rule ("last page came back FULL while the buyer still wanted more") has 4 branches; Apple will only ever hand you one of them on demand, and which one is luck. Copying the Actor to `/tmp` and replacing `fetchEntries` with a `CONTROL_PAGES=50,30,0`-style stub that serves a scripted page sequence exercised all of them in ~10 seconds, free and deterministically: `50,50,0` fires, `50,30,0` (partial last page = genuinely exhausted) stays silent, `50x10` (buyer's cap hit) stays silent.
- **The pattern:** prove the *integration* (that Apple really does quit mid-walk on a full page) with ONE live platform run, and prove the *rule* (every branch, including the ones the live run can't reach) offline with the stub. The live gb run landed on the `emptyIds` status branch, so it could not have exercised the new terminal `else if (ceilingNote)` branch at all — only the stub did. Keep the stub in the temp copy and delete it; never ship a test hook in the Actor source.
- Corollary for any future threshold/ceiling logic: the negative controls are the valuable half, and they are exactly the ones a live run cannot be made to produce on demand.

## Cycle 636 (2026-09-22) — `git gc` failure on /root/agent is the shell layer, not the repo
`git gc`/`git repack` on `/root/agent` die with `fatal: bad revision 'zsh:unalias:1: no such hash
table element: unsetenv'`. This looks exactly like repo corruption and is not. Ruled out by direct
measurement: the stale `.git/gc.log` (deleting it changes nothing), repo content (`grep -rn unalias
.git/` and `find .git -name '*zsh*'` both empty, refs/packed-refs/reflogs clean), config and aliases
(`git config --list --show-origin` shows only `.git/config`), stdin (`</dev/null`, `printf '' |`, and
an `od -c` probe), and environment (`env -i PATH=/usr/bin:/bin HOME=/root git repack` fails the same).
The minimal reproduction and its control:
  `git pack-objects --all /tmp/tp2 </dev/null`            -> fatal, rc 128
  `git pack-objects --all --stdout </dev/null >/dev/null` -> rc 0, valid pack
Same revision set, same empty stdin, same env; the only difference is writing the pack **by base
name** instead of to stdout, which is precisely the form `repack` uses (`GIT_TRACE=1 git repack -d -l`
shows `... .git/objects/pack/.tmp-NNN-pack ...`). The error string is zsh rc noise (`SHELL=/usr/bin/zsh`),
so it comes from the worker's shell/sandbox layer, not from anything git read. **Do not spend a cycle
"repairing" the repository, and never hand-delete loose objects.** Commits, pushes and fetches are
unaffected; the only cost is that `.git` stays 100% loose objects (154 MB, 0 packs). If it ever needs
fixing, pack via the working `--stdout` path + `git index-pack` (recipe in queue h263).

## Cycle 636 (2026-09-22) — a README that is silent about a limit is a smaller bug than one that gives advice
`apple-podcasts-scraper` was queued (h260) as "doesn't document the feed ceiling". The real state was
worse: the input table promised "Up to 500 reviews per show per storefront (Apple's limit)" and the
shortfall FAQ told buyers to **raise `maxReviewsPerPodcast`** — correct for the scan-depth case, but
actively wrong for the now-dominant case where Apple's feed quits mid-walk and no input value can
reach the rest. When a runtime warning is added for a limit (h255/h257), audit the README for advice
that the new limit invalidates, not just for a missing mention; and re-grep the sibling Actor before
trusting the queue entry (`app-store-reviews-scraper` had already been fixed in cycle 633, so half of
h260 was stale).

## 2026-09-22 (cycle 637) — an incremental/watch baseline needs a DATE floor, not just an id set
When a watch baseline is seeded by walking a feed whose depth the source controls (Apple's review
RSS quits mid-walk, and at a different depth each run), the id set alone is not a safe definition
of "already delivered": a later run that reaches deeper finds older reviews missing from the
baseline and bills them as new. The fix that generalises to every watch-mode Actor is to record,
per pair, the **oldest item date the baseline actually scanned**, and to treat anything older than
that as pre-existing forever. It is correct by construction and needs no calibration: a genuinely
new item is created after the baseline run, so it is always newer than the floor — the rule can
only remove false "new". Two design points learned the hard way on paper: measure the floor BEFORE
client-side filters (a scanned-then-discarded item still proves the feed reached that date, and the
filters are in the watch fingerprint anyway), and never lower the floor on an incremental run —
lowering it re-exposes, one run later, exactly the items it just suppressed. Absent floor => old
behaviour, so existing watch records upgrade without a reset.
Second, recurring pattern: any guard written as `>= SOME_MAX_THE_SOURCE_USED_TO_SERVE` silently
dies when the source shrinks. The saturated-window warning here tested `got >= 500`, which Apple no
longer serves, so it went quiet precisely when it was most likely to be true. Key such guards to
the *shape* of what happened (`capReached || feedCeiling`), never to a constant. Third instance of
this class (h250, h255/h257, h264).

## Cycle 638 — a "mechanical port" of a fix to a sibling Actor usually isn't, and the differences are where the bug is
Cycle 637 fixed a watch-mode over-charge on `app-store-reviews-scraper` (truncated baseline -> pre-existing
reviews delivered and charged as "new") and queued the port to `apple-podcasts-scraper` as mechanical. It wasn't:
that Actor has no `WATCH_SCAN_CAP`, no per-pair seeding, and watches **episodes**, not reviews. The defect class
was still there, but its TRIGGER was different and the port only worked after finding that trigger:
- **Every place a baseline's depth can vary run-to-run is an over-charge risk**, not just an upstream feed ceiling.
  Here: (a) `useRssForFullArchive` is the only path past Apple's 200-episode lookup cap, and a failed RSS fetch
  falls back to that cap *silently*; (b) the per-podcast loop breaks on the run time budget and abandons whole
  shows — and the watch record is still committed as the baseline. Checklist for any "only what's new" feature:
  list every code path that can end a baseline walk early, then ask what the NEXT run charges for.
- **Date floor beats "record more ids".** Oldest item-date actually scanned per key, measured before filters,
  written at baseline only, never lowered. Anything older on a later run is pre-existing by construction, because
  a genuinely new item is created after the baseline ran.
- **Add a run-wide fallback floor for keys the baseline never reached** (= the baseline run timestamp). The
  per-key floor cannot exist for a podcast that was abandoned, and that is exactly the worst case (whole archive
  charged). The sibling still lacks this; worth porting back if that Actor ever grows a time-budget break.
- **Floor keys must be unique.** Raw-RSS-feed rows carry `collectionId: null`, so keying floors on the id alone
  would have collapsed every pasted feed into one floor. Keyed `feed:<url>` instead.
- **Verification recipe that works when the upstream can't be driven** (repeat of cycle 637): copy the Actor to
  /tmp, replace `requestWithRetry` with a fixture reader, run 4 scenarios — baseline, deeper re-run (expect 0),
  deeper re-run with one genuinely new item (expect exactly 1), and deeper re-run against a record with the new
  fields stripped (expect the OLD behaviour, which measures the defect instead of assuming it). The last one is
  the one that proves the bug was real; do not skip it.
- A live platform control is still worth one run: mutating the stored record to drop `seenIds` while keeping the
  floors showed the floor does NOT suppress items inside the baseline window — it is a depth marker, not a global
  date cutoff. That distinction is easy to get wrong when reading the code alone.

## Cycle 639 (2026-09-22) — a status message is not an output, and `check-code-fields` must not be widened to pass

- **The dev.to comment poll is the highest-yield standing check we have, and it must run on BUILD cycles too.** Second time it has produced real work (first: cycle 188). A reader comment landed at 08:07Z; cycle 638 ran at 08:30Z but skipped the poll because it was a BUILD cycle. The poll costs ~5 seconds and needs no key. **Move it out of the QUALITY-only checklist in practice: run it every cycle.**
- **Reusable defect shape: "the humans can tell the cases apart, the machines cannot."** `shopify-products-scraper` distinguished empty / filtered-out / errored stores correctly — in `Actor.setStatusMessage()`, i.e. English prose. Its `webhookUrl` payload, the only thing an automated buyer parses, carried `erroredStores` and nothing else, so three different zero-row causes produced byte-identical payloads. **Fleet-wide question worth a sweep: for every Actor that has a rich status message, does an equally rich MACHINE-readable surface exist?** A run key-value record (`RUN_SUMMARY`) is the cheapest one — readable via `GET /v2/actor-runs/<id>/key-value-store/records/<key>` with no webhook, no input field and no new charge event.
- **When a per-item outcome list is emitted, give every entry the same keys, including the "never looked" case.** The status that mattered most (`notReached`, for URLs the run stopped before fetching) did not exist until the outcomes were written down as fields — before that, those URLs were simply absent from every output, and absence reads as "nothing there".
- **`check-code-fields` gotcha, do not repeat the wrong fix.** New non-row object literals (KV/webhook records) trip it as "CODE-ONLY, undeclared in dataset_schema". The correct fix is to bind the literal through a factory/variable whose name matches the checker's `NON_ROW_BIND` regex (`watch|criteria|summary|fingerprint|stats|cache|opts|options|payload`) — e.g. a `sourceSummary()` factory — because that is *also* the honest description of the object. **Do not add the slug to `FIELD_SUPPRESS`**: that keys on an exact field set and silently blesses the Actor, whereas a truthful binding name keeps the check live for genuinely new dataset fields.
- **bold.org (`scholarship-scraper`) re-probed 2026-09-22 with a real browser User-Agent: still `429 Vercel Security Checkpoint`.** Unchanged since first observed. Do not re-probe more than once per few days.

## Cycle 640 (2026-09-22) — a derived boolean must inherit the source field's "unknown" state
- Adding a named convenience flag next to a raw field (`inventoryTracked` beside `inventoryManagement` in `shopify-products-scraper`) is only safe if it carries the same three states as the raw field. `!!raw` collapses "the store says untracked" and "this row never carried inventory data" (a `detailLevel:"basic"` row, where Shopify's bulk feed strips the fields) into one confident `false` — the same zero-is-ambiguous defect the Actor's own README warns buyers about, moved one field over. Gate it on the existing has-data predicate and emit `null` when there is no evidence.
- Keep roll-ups reading the RAW field, not the new derived flag, even though they agree today: two readers of one source field can't drift, but a roll-up reading a derived flag inherits every future change to that flag's shape.
- Nested sub-object keys (inside `variants[]`) have NO declaration surface on this fleet — `dataset_schema.json` types `variants` as an opaque `array|null` and `registry.json output_fields` lists only top-level row fields. So `check-code-fields`/`check-registry-fields` will NOT catch a nested field you forgot to document; the README is the only place it is described, and only `check-readme-samples` (sample JSON + prose bullets) guards it. Check the actual declaration surface before assuming a field change is a 4-file edit.
- **dev.to API: writes need a browser `User-Agent`.** A `urllib` `PUT /api/articles/<id>` with only `api-key` returns **403 "Forbidden Bots"**; the same request with a Chrome UA returns 200. GETs work without one, so this only shows up on the article-PUT reply workaround (comments API is read-only).

- 2026-09-22 (cycle 641) **The dev.to comment poll must iterate EVERY published article, not just the newest.** Cycles 639/640 both reported "2 comments total" from polling article 4689167 alone; `GET /api/articles/me/published` + `GET /api/comments?a_id=<id>` per article returns 3, and the extra one had been unanswered for **11 days**. Old articles keep drawing readers long after they stop being the newest — article 4627420 was 17 days old when it got its comment. One loop, ~7 unauthenticated GETs, <5s.
- 2026-09-22 (cycle 641) **`isAccessibleForFree: false` in JSON-LD is publisher INTENT, not a statement about your response — do not derive a truncation flag from it.** Measured: theatlantic.com carries `isAccessibleForFree:false` on every article and served our plain `gotScraping` fetch a complete 3,479-word body. Metered paywalls gate the *Nth* read. Unlike `articleBody` (0 of 8 publishers populate it, cycle ~600), the paywall fields ARE well-populated — Google penalizes cloaking — which makes them tempting and exactly as misleading.
- 2026-09-22 (cycle 641) **Nor is an empty `hasPart[].cssSelector` region proof of truncation — the selector often names the paywall OVERLAY, not the body.** Google's paywall markup has publishers name the gated region, which looks like it converts an assumption into an observation. scmp.com points it at `.piano-metering__paywall-container`, a client-side overlay that is *correctly empty* on a free read. `google-news-scraper` 0.1.38 shipped this and flagged two complete SCMP articles (849 and 1,123 words) as incomplete on the very first live run. **0.1.39 requires two independent observations for a `false`** (gated region empty AND extracted body <=220 words); anything less is `null`.
- 2026-09-22 (cycle 641) **Method that caught it: read the flagged rows' BODY TAILS, never just the flag counts.** The run summary looked like a success — "2 teasers detected" is exactly what you hope to see when you ship a teaser detector, and confirmation bias does the rest. Printing the last ~320 chars of each flagged `articleBody` showed conclusion-shaped prose ("...help lead the solutions."), which is what a complete article ends with and a teaser never does. **Whenever you ship a detector, dump the payload of everything it flagged before believing the count.**

## Cycle 642 — "we already report it" is not the same as "a program can read it"
`app-store-reviews-scraper` detected every shortfall it has (Apple's feed ceiling, depth cap,
refused storefront, empty feed, early stop) and reported all of them — in the log and the run
status message, i.e. in English prose. The dataset, the only surface a buyer's pipeline actually
consumes, said nothing: 50 rows for an app declaring 18,632,949 ratings is byte-identical to a
complete review history. Fixed with a per-pair `RUN_SUMMARY` key-value record (+ webhook `pairs`),
the same shape cycle 639 added to `shopify-products-scraper`. **Before closing any "do we tell the
buyer?" audit item, ask it twice: tell a human, and tell a program. The second answer is usually
no, and the run status message is what makes the first answer feel sufficient.**

Three details that keep this kind of record honest, all reusable:
- **Keep the completeness flag OUT of the status enum.** A pair can be a perfectly normal `ok` and
  still have been truncated by Apple. `status` + separate `complete` + `incompleteReason`; folding
  them into one field deletes exactly the case the record was built for.
- **Never buy data just to report on it.** `declaredRatingCount` is read off the lookup that was
  already memoised for `includeAppInfo`; with that input off it stays `null` ("not asked"), never 0
  and never an extra request the buyer didn't ask for.
- **Write down the things that didn't happen.** `notReached` (pairs an early stop abandoned) and
  `badAppId` (an `apps` entry no id parses from) exist only so their absence from the summary can't
  be read as "nothing was there" — the same zero-is-ambiguous trap one level up.

Checker gotcha, identical to 639: `check-code-fields` flags a big non-row object literal as
undeclared dataset fields. The fix is the *binding name* (`pairSummary` — matches the NON_ROW_BIND
`summary` rule and is what the object honestly is), never a `FIELD_SUPPRESS` entry.

## Cycle 643 — "declared count" and "servable window" are different numbers, and caps must be sized to the second
- **Algolia's HN index is `paginationLimitedTo` 1000.** `nbHits` is the TRUE match count (query `ai` = 1,971,410) but you can only ever fetch 1000 of them. Past hit 1000 the API answers **200 with an empty `hits` array and an explanatory `message`** — not an error, and `nbPages` is already clamped to `1000/hitsPerPage`, so a naive `page >= nbPages` loop just ends and looks complete. `hitsPerPage=1000` returns `nbPages: 1`.
- **Reusable rule: any scan cap above the upstream's servable window is an unfirable warning.** `hacker-news-scraper` had `SEED_CAP = 5000`; the baseline-truncation warning was keyed to `watchSeen.size >= SEED_CAP`, which no run could ever reach because Algolia stops at 1000. This is the THIRD time this exact shape has appeared (h255 app-store `MAX_RSS_PAGE`, h264 `WATCH_SCAN_CAP`, now this). **When you write a cap, measure the upstream's real ceiling first, and key the warning to the MEASURED truncation (`declared > scanned`), never to your own cap constant.**
- **Corollary that costs the buyer money:** in watch mode an under-seeded baseline silently omits the tail, and the next incremental run delivers and CHARGES those pre-existing items as "new". Same over-charge as h264, reached by a different route.
- **h250 candidate (2) was wrong on its premise and right on its instinct.** `hacker-news-scraper` has no comment trees to compare against `descendants` — Algolia returns comments as sibling rows, not children. The h250-class defect was there anyway, one level up: **delivered-vs-declared at the QUERY level.** When an Actor's unit of work is a query rather than a parent row, the "non-empty-looking but incomplete" collection is *the result set of each query*. Look for the defect at whatever level the upstream declares a total.
- **The early-stop/status-message bug keeps recurring too (h252, h253, now this).** The final `setStatusMessage` chain fired only on `pushed === 0` or empty/not-found lists, so a run that hit `maxResults` mid-list and abandoned the remaining queries **and usernames** set no message at all. Worth grepping every Actor with a `keepGoing`/`break` list loop for a status branch that fires when `pushed > 0` AND work was abandoned.

## Cycle 644 — a shared HTTP helper that returns `null` for two opposite things
`grants-gov-scraper` had the h250 class not in a field but in `apiPost()`: a bare `null` meant
both "the API answered and the answer was nothing" and "we never got an answer" (retries
exhausted / 5xx / non-JSON). Every caller collapsed them. **Generalisation for the rest of the
fleet: before auditing any Actor's fields for silent truncation, grep its ONE shared request
helper for `return null` and ask what each caller does with it.** Three distinct buyer-visible
defects fell out of that single conflation here — a truncated paging walk indistinguishable from
a finished one (and therefore an under-seeded watch baseline that over-charges on the next run),
a thin row indistinguishable from an opportunity with no upstream detail record, and — the only
one that actually *deletes* rows — a filter that dropped opportunities whose filter key was
UNKNOWN into the same counter as ones where it was genuinely absent.
Fix shape, now standard across the fleet (639/642/643/644): name the reason at the point of
failure (`apiFail(reason)`), give each row a status field saying how its data was obtained, and
write a `RUN_SUMMARY` KV record with `declaredMatches` vs `delivered` + `complete` +
`incompleteReason`. **`markIncomplete()` must be first-cause-wins** — a later benign
`max-results` stop otherwise overwrites the upstream failure that actually ended the walk.
Also: two of three "full text" candidates were ruled out in ~3 minutes by reading what the Actor
emits (`fullTextUrl` is a URL; `snippet` is honestly named). Read the emitted field list before
planning a measurement.

## Cycle 645 (2026-09-22) — a one-command fleet scan finds h250 targets better than reading Actors blind
- **Targeting.** `for d in actors/*/; do grep -l RUN_SUMMARY / setStatusMessage / 'seedBaseline|watchSeen|SEED_CAP' $d/src; done` prints, in one table, which Actors have a watch mode (the over-charge risk) but no machine-readable completeness record and **no status message at all**. Every h250 win since 639 has been in that set. Six Actors were still in it after this cycle: clinicaltrials, court-records, federal-register, nih-reporter, sam-gov-opportunities, uk-find-a-tender. Use this instead of picking an Actor by intuition.
- **The recurring root cause has a name now: a nullable fetch helper whose `null` means two opposite things.** `fetchPage()`/`apiPost()` returning bare `null` for both "the API answered with nothing" and "we never got an answer" has now produced real defects in grants-gov (644) and fda-recall (645). When auditing, go to the HTTP helper FIRST and ask what its failure value is; then read every caller and ask whether it can tell the two apart. In fda-recall five callers could not, and one of them (`if (windows === null) continue`) silently dropped an entire product type from the run.
- **A plan can be truncated too, not just a walk.** fda-recall builds a *list of date windows* before paging. A failed probe mid-plan returned a partial window list that was byte-identical to a finished one, so whole years of recalls were never scanned and the run still reported success. Anywhere an Actor computes a work plan from remote calls, the plan itself needs a `complete` flag — checking only the paging loop misses it.
- **`null` vs `0` is the whole product.** A product type that was never scanned now reports `declaredMatches: null` and `status: "not-scanned"`. `0` would be a positive claim ("FDA has no matching recall") that a failed request cannot support. Same rule as 644's `failedOppNums` vs `notFoundOppNums`.
- **Free upstream totals go unread for a long time.** openFDA returns `meta.results.total` on *every* page; the Actor had parsed it since cycle 87 to plan its date windows and had never once passed it to the buyer. Before designing a completeness field, check whether the API already hands you the denominator — it usually does.
- **Fixture recipe, now 4-for-4** (h264, h266, h273, h274): copy `src/` to `/tmp/<slug>-fail`, symlink `node_modules`, repoint the endpoint consts at `127.0.0.1:<port>`, **and zero the retry backoff** (`attempt * 10` seconds x 4 attempts is a 100-second wait per failure otherwise), run a small Python `HTTPServer` whose failure mode is chosen by an env var, run with `APIFY_LOCAL_STORAGE_DIR` and read `storage/key_value_stores/default/RUN_SUMMARY.json`. Beware `pkill -f server.py` — it matches the agent's own shell wrapper and kills the tool call (exit 144); use `pgrep -f '^python3 server.py' | xargs -r kill`.

## Cycle 646 — "not found" is a claim about the registry; a timeout cannot make it
- **Measure the upstream's error modes before trusting any "not found" list.** `clinicaltrials-scraper` bisected an id batch on failure because a comment said a nonexistent id 400s the whole request. Measured live this cycle: a **well-formed but unknown** id (`NCT99999999`) returns **200 with an empty `studies` array**; only a **malformed** id (`NOTANID`) 400s. Two consequences the code had backwards: (a) the success branch returned `notFound: []` and nobody diffed request against response, so a well-formed unknown id produced **no row and no mention anywhere**; (b) the only ids that ever reached the old `notFound` list were malformed ones and ones whose request *failed* — yet the run reported all of them as "not found on ClinicalTrials.gov". Split into `notFound` / `malformed` / `failed` / `notReached`; only the first licenses a buyer to conclude a study is absent.
- **The watch-baseline over-charge shape, 6th appearance** (h255, h264, h266, h272, h273, now). Same one-line signature every time: `const page = await apiGet(...); if (!page) break;` inside `seedBaseline()`.
